========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… what finally pushed me to come in is I can’t keep my focus the way I need to anymore. I’m an electrician, so a lot of my day is estimates, code forms, keeping track of what materials go with which job, that kind of thing—and lately I’ll sit down to finish one estimate and then a tool noise in the shop, or my phone buzzing, or even just some random new idea I get about reorganizing the workshop will pull me completely off track.

And then I’ll realize I’ve mixed up parts of two jobs, or I started a form and wandered off into something else before finishing it. It’s not just “oh, I got distracted once.” It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to mess with my work, and that’s what scared me into making the appointment.

Yeah—restless is probably the right word. I don’t know if it always looks like fidgeting exactly, but I feel like I’m constantly in motion. I’ll go from a job site to doing a bid, then back to the workshop to rewire some test panel I suddenly decided needs fixing, and then I’ll start sketching out some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one.

It’s like sitting still feels… wrong, almost like I’m wasting time if I’m not doing something useful. Even when I’m supposed to just sit and finish paperwork, my body wants to get up and check tools, move materials around, start another task—something. It’s gotten to where I’m doing a lot, but not necessarily finishing it cleanly.

I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can think back and remember being a kid and not loving sitting still in class, but I don’t know if that was “trouble” in the way you mean, or just being a normal kid who wanted to be outside or doing something with his hands.

I don’t have, like, a clear memory of teachers constantly saying, “He can’t focus,” or anything that stands out strongly. School was kind of school—I got through it. I know I was always more interested in practical stuff, building things, taking things apart, that kind of thing, but I don’t know if I’d call that restlessness exactly.

So… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.

I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.

Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.

So I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.

At work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.

And with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.

At home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.

So yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.

I’m an electrician, so a lot of my day is estimates, code forms, keeping track of what materials go with which job, that kind of thing—and lately I’ll sit down to finish one estimate and then a tool noise in the shop, or my phone buzzing, or even just some random new idea I get about reorganizing the workshop will pull me completely off track.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve mixed up parts of two jobs, or I started a form and wandered off into something else before finishing it. It’s not just “oh, I got distracted once.” It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to mess with my work, and that’s what scared me into making the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah—restless is probably the right word. I don’t know if it always looks like fidgeting exactly, but I feel like I’m constantly in motion. I’ll go from a job site to doing a bid, then back to the workshop to rewire some test panel I suddenly decided needs fixing, and then I’ll start sketching out some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one.\n\nIt’s like sitting still feels… wrong, almost like I’m wasting time if I’m not doing something useful. Even when I’m supposed to just sit and finish paperwork, my body wants to get up and check tools, move materials around, start another task—something. It’s gotten to where I’m doing a lot, but not necessarily finishing it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or restlessness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can think back and remember being a kid and not loving sitting still in class, but I don’t know if that was “trouble” in the way you mean, or just being a normal kid who wanted to be outside or doing something with his hands.\n\nI don’t have, like, a clear memory of teachers constantly saying, “He can’t focus,” or anything that stands out strongly. School was kind of school—I got through it. I know I was always more interested in practical stuff, building things, taking things apart, that kind of thing, but I don’t know if I’d call that restlessness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or family members ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember them saying much in a clear way. Like, I’m trying to think of the exact kinds of comments my parents made, and nothing really specific comes to mind—nothing like, “You were bouncing off the walls,” or “You never sat still,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nI mean, families say little things, you know? Like maybe I was “always busy” or I liked being outside or messing with stuff, but I don’t know if that was them describing a problem or just normal kid stuff. My dad was pretty practical too, so if I was taking something apart or helping with a project, that might’ve just been seen as useful, not hyperactive or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no for sure, because maybe they did and I just didn’t register it, but I don’t have a strong memory of my family making comments about my activity level being unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and restlessness have been present throughout your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.\n\nIt lines up pretty closely with when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That was a big step, and since then it’s felt like my brain just doesn’t shut off the same way. Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and restlessness caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.\n\nAt work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.\n\nAnd with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.\n\nAt home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what finally pushed me to come in is I can’t keep my focus the way I need to anymore. I’m an electrician, so a lot of my day is estimates, code forms, keeping track of what materials go with which job, that kind of thing—and lately I’ll sit down to finish one estimate and then a tool noise in the shop, or my phone buzzing, or even just some random new idea I get about reorganizing the workshop will pull me completely off track.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve mixed up parts of two jobs, or I started a form and wandered off into something else before finishing it. It’s not just “oh, I got distracted once.” It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to mess with my work, and that’s what scared me into making the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah—restless is probably the right word. I don’t know if it always looks like fidgeting exactly, but I feel like I’m constantly in motion. I’ll go from a job site to doing a bid, then back to the workshop to rewire some test panel I suddenly decided needs fixing, and then I’ll start sketching out some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one.\n\nIt’s like sitting still feels… wrong, almost like I’m wasting time if I’m not doing something useful. Even when I’m supposed to just sit and finish paperwork, my body wants to get up and check tools, move materials around, start another task—something. It’s gotten to where I’m doing a lot, but not necessarily finishing it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or restlessness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can think back and remember being a kid and not loving sitting still in class, but I don’t know if that was “trouble” in the way you mean, or just being a normal kid who wanted to be outside or doing something with his hands.\n\nI don’t have, like, a clear memory of teachers constantly saying, “He can’t focus,” or anything that stands out strongly. School was kind of school—I got through it. I know I was always more interested in practical stuff, building things, taking things apart, that kind of thing, but I don’t know if I’d call that restlessness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or family members ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember them saying much in a clear way. Like, I’m trying to think of the exact kinds of comments my parents made, and nothing really specific comes to mind—nothing like, “You were bouncing off the walls,” or “You never sat still,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nI mean, families say little things, you know? Like maybe I was “always busy” or I liked being outside or messing with stuff, but I don’t know if that was them describing a problem or just normal kid stuff. My dad was pretty practical too, so if I was taking something apart or helping with a project, that might’ve just been seen as useful, not hyperactive or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no for sure, because maybe they did and I just didn’t register it, but I don’t have a strong memory of my family making comments about my activity level being unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and restlessness have been present throughout your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.\n\nIt lines up pretty closely with when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That was a big step, and since then it’s felt like my brain just doesn’t shut off the same way. Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and restlessness caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.\n\nAt work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.\n\nAnd with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.\n\nAt home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can really point to. I don’t remember having this kind of focus problem or restlessness before I was 12.\n\nI mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy, but this thing I’m talking about now feels different. It’s been building more in the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I started noticing it as something that was actually affecting me, not something I remember being part of my childhood.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean… as certain as I can be, looking back that far. I don’t have some perfect memory of being a kid, so I don’t want to say it like I’ve reviewed every moment of my childhood. But no, I can’t really identify this same kind of thing before age 12.

What I’m dealing with now feels tied to the last several months. It’s been building for about seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I really noticed my focus and restlessness becoming something I couldn’t just brush off or explain as normal busyness.

Before that, I’m sure I had normal kid energy or got distracted sometimes like anyone, but I don’t remember it being this pattern or this level of problem. The clear change, to me, is much more recent.

Before age 7? I really don’t think so. I know I’ve said something similar already, but when I try to look back that far, I just don’t have a memory of being that kind of distracted or restless as a little kid in a way that stood out.

It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or family members ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember them saying much in a clear way. Like, I’m trying to think of the exact kinds of comments my parents made, and nothing really specific comes to mind—nothing like, “You were bouncing off the walls,” or “You never sat still,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nI mean, families say little things, you know? Like maybe I was “always busy” or I liked being outside or messing with stuff, but I don’t know if that was them describing a problem or just normal kid stuff. My dad was pretty practical too, so if I was taking something apart or helping with a project, that might’ve just been seen as useful, not hyperactive or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no for sure, because maybe they did and I just didn’t register it, but I don’t have a strong memory of my family making comments about my activity level being unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and restlessness have been present throughout your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.\n\nIt lines up pretty closely with when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That was a big step, and since then it’s felt like my brain just doesn’t shut off the same way. Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and restlessness caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.\n\nAt work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.\n\nAnd with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.\n\nAt home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can really point to. I don’t remember having this kind of focus problem or restlessness before I was 12.\n\nI mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy, but this thing I’m talking about now feels different. It’s been building more in the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I started noticing it as something that was actually affecting me, not something I remember being part of my childhood.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I mean… as certain as I can be, looking back that far. I don’t have some perfect memory of being a kid, so I don’t want to say it like I’ve reviewed every moment of my childhood. But no, I can’t really identify this same kind of thing before age 12.\n\nWhat I’m dealing with now feels tied to the last several months. It’s been building for about seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I really noticed my focus and restlessness becoming something I couldn’t just brush off or explain as normal busyness.\n\nBefore that, I’m sure I had normal kid energy or got distracted sometimes like anyone, but I don’t remember it being this pattern or this level of problem. The clear change, to me, is much more recent.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain that you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity at any point before the age of 7?\n\nPatient: Before age 7? I really don’t think so. I know I’ve said something similar already, but when I try to look back that far, I just don’t have a memory of being that kind of distracted or restless as a little kid in a way that stood out.\n\nWhat I can say pretty clearly is that this current thing has been building for about seven months. It started becoming noticeable after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment, and that’s when it went from just normal stress or being busy to something I actually felt was affecting me day to day.\n\nSo, no—I can’t honestly point to anything before age 7. The timeline that makes sense to me is much more recent, around those last seven months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as these focus and restlessness problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been stressed, definitely, and the timing kind of overlaps because the focus and restlessness stuff started around a period where there was more pressure in general. But as far as whether it was like a separate depression or anxiety issue… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I don’t want to say yes to something if what I’m describing is just being overwhelmed or under pressure. I’ve noticed the focus and restlessness more clearly than anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus and restlessness issues might be related to feeling overwhelmed or anxious about your work and personal life situation?\n\nPatient: It might be, yeah… I mean, I can see how it could be connected. I just don’t know how to separate it out neatly.\n\nWork and personal stuff have felt like a lot, and when there’s that kind of pressure, I do notice I get more keyed up and it’s harder to settle into one thing. But I’m not sure if that means I’m anxious in the way you’re asking, or if I’m just reacting to having too much on my plate. Like, I don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, it’s definitely anxiety,” because I’m not totally sure that’s what it is.\n\nWhat I do know is that when I feel overwhelmed, the focus issues and restlessness seem harder to ignore. Whether that’s the cause, or it’s just making something else worse, I honestly don’t know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely certain? I don’t know if I can say completely certain, just because that’s a long time ago and memory gets fuzzy. Before 12 is still pretty far back.

But I don’t really remember having those kinds of problems then. I wasn’t thinking of myself as a kid who couldn’t sit still or couldn’t pay attention, at least not in any way that stands out to me now. I’m trying to be careful, because maybe there were normal kid moments—getting bored, not wanting to do homework, whatever—but nothing that I can point to and say, “Yes, that was a real pattern before I turned 12.”

So I guess my honest answer is: I’m not completely certain in the absolute sense, but I don’t have a clear memory of inattention or hyperactivity being an issue before my 12th birthday.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what finally pushed me to come in is I can’t keep my focus the way I need to anymore. I’m an electrician, so a lot of my day is estimates, code forms, keeping track of what materials go with which job, that kind of thing—and lately I’ll sit down to finish one estimate and then a tool noise in the shop, or my phone buzzing, or even just some random new idea I get about reorganizing the workshop will pull me completely off track.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve mixed up parts of two jobs, or I started a form and wandered off into something else before finishing it. It’s not just “oh, I got distracted once.” It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to mess with my work, and that’s what scared me into making the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah—restless is probably the right word. I don’t know if it always looks like fidgeting exactly, but I feel like I’m constantly in motion. I’ll go from a job site to doing a bid, then back to the workshop to rewire some test panel I suddenly decided needs fixing, and then I’ll start sketching out some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one.\n\nIt’s like sitting still feels… wrong, almost like I’m wasting time if I’m not doing something useful. Even when I’m supposed to just sit and finish paperwork, my body wants to get up and check tools, move materials around, start another task—something. It’s gotten to where I’m doing a lot, but not necessarily finishing it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or restlessness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can think back and remember being a kid and not loving sitting still in class, but I don’t know if that was “trouble” in the way you mean, or just being a normal kid who wanted to be outside or doing something with his hands.\n\nI don’t have, like, a clear memory of teachers constantly saying, “He can’t focus,” or anything that stands out strongly. School was kind of school—I got through it. I know I was always more interested in practical stuff, building things, taking things apart, that kind of thing, but I don’t know if I’d call that restlessness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or family members ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember them saying much in a clear way. Like, I’m trying to think of the exact kinds of comments my parents made, and nothing really specific comes to mind—nothing like, “You were bouncing off the walls,” or “You never sat still,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nI mean, families say little things, you know? Like maybe I was “always busy” or I liked being outside or messing with stuff, but I don’t know if that was them describing a problem or just normal kid stuff. My dad was pretty practical too, so if I was taking something apart or helping with a project, that might’ve just been seen as useful, not hyperactive or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no for sure, because maybe they did and I just didn’t register it, but I don’t have a strong memory of my family making comments about my activity level being unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and restlessness have been present throughout your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.\n\nIt lines up pretty closely with when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That was a big step, and since then it’s felt like my brain just doesn’t shut off the same way. Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and restlessness caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.\n\nAt work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.\n\nAnd with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.\n\nAt home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can really point to. I don’t remember having this kind of focus problem or restlessness before I was 12.\n\nI mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy, but this thing I’m talking about now feels different. It’s been building more in the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I started noticing it as something that was actually affecting me, not something I remember being part of my childhood.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I mean… as certain as I can be, looking back that far. I don’t have some perfect memory of being a kid, so I don’t want to say it like I’ve reviewed every moment of my childhood. But no, I can’t really identify this same kind of thing before age 12.\n\nWhat I’m dealing with now feels tied to the last several months. It’s been building for about seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I really noticed my focus and restlessness becoming something I couldn’t just brush off or explain as normal busyness.\n\nBefore that, I’m sure I had normal kid energy or got distracted sometimes like anyone, but I don’t remember it being this pattern or this level of problem. The clear change, to me, is much more recent.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain that you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity at any point before the age of 7?\n\nPatient: Before age 7? I really don’t think so. I know I’ve said something similar already, but when I try to look back that far, I just don’t have a memory of being that kind of distracted or restless as a little kid in a way that stood out.\n\nWhat I can say pretty clearly is that this current thing has been building for about seven months. It started becoming noticeable after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment, and that’s when it went from just normal stress or being busy to something I actually felt was affecting me day to day.\n\nSo, no—I can’t honestly point to anything before age 7. The timeline that makes sense to me is much more recent, around those last seven months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as these focus and restlessness problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been stressed, definitely, and the timing kind of overlaps because the focus and restlessness stuff started around a period where there was more pressure in general. But as far as whether it was like a separate depression or anxiety issue… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I don’t want to say yes to something if what I’m describing is just being overwhelmed or under pressure. I’ve noticed the focus and restlessness more clearly than anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus and restlessness issues might be related to feeling overwhelmed or anxious about your work and personal life situation?\n\nPatient: It might be, yeah… I mean, I can see how it could be connected. I just don’t know how to separate it out neatly.\n\nWork and personal stuff have felt like a lot, and when there’s that kind of pressure, I do notice I get more keyed up and it’s harder to settle into one thing. But I’m not sure if that means I’m anxious in the way you’re asking, or if I’m just reacting to having too much on my plate. Like, I don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, it’s definitely anxiety,” because I’m not totally sure that’s what it is.\n\nWhat I do know is that when I feel overwhelmed, the focus issues and restlessness seem harder to ignore. Whether that’s the cause, or it’s just making something else worse, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Are you completely certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity at any point before your 12th birthday?\n\nPatient: Completely certain? But as far as whether it was like a separate depression or anxiety issue… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. Whether that’s the cause, or it’s just making something else worse, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Are you completely certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity at any point before your 12th birthday?\n\nPatient: Completely certain? I don’t know if I can say completely certain, just because that’s a long time ago and memory gets fuzzy. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I wouldn’t say I’m certain in the way you’re asking, no. I mean, I don’t have some clear record in my head from being 8 or 9 years old where I can confidently say, “No, that never happened.” That feels too absolute.

What I can say is I don’t remember inattention being a major issue before 12. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers constantly saying I couldn’t focus, or me feeling like I was always unable to pay attention in a way that stood out. But childhood memories are kind of blurry, and I’m also aware that sometimes you don’t label things that way when you’re a kid.

So… no, I’m not certain. I just don’t have anything specific I can point to from before age 12 that makes me think, yes, that was definitely happening then.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what finally pushed me to come in is I can’t keep my focus the way I need to anymore. It’s not just “oh, I got distracted once.” It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to mess with my work, and that’s what scared me into making the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah—restless is probably the right word. I don’t know if it always looks like fidgeting exactly, but I feel like I’m constantly in motion. I’ll go from a job site to doing a bid, then back to the workshop to rewire some test panel I suddenly decided needs fixing, and then I’ll start sketching out some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one.\n\nIt’s like sitting still feels… wrong, almost like I’m wasting time if I’m not doing something useful. Even when I’m supposed to just sit and finish paperwork, my body wants to get up and check tools, move materials around, start another task—something. It’s gotten to where I’m doing a lot, but not necessarily finishing it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or restlessness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can think back and remember being a kid and not loving sitting still in class, but I don’t know if that was “trouble” in the way you mean, or just being a normal kid who wanted to be outside or doing something with his hands.\n\nI don’t have, like, a clear memory of teachers constantly saying, “He can’t focus,” or anything that stands out strongly. School was kind of school—I got through it. I know I was always more interested in practical stuff, building things, taking things apart, that kind of thing, but I don’t know if I’d call that restlessness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or family members ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember them saying much in a clear way. Like, I’m trying to think of the exact kinds of comments my parents made, and nothing really specific comes to mind—nothing like, “You were bouncing off the walls,” or “You never sat still,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nI mean, families say little things, you know? Like maybe I was “always busy” or I liked being outside or messing with stuff, but I don’t know if that was them describing a problem or just normal kid stuff. My dad was pretty practical too, so if I was taking something apart or helping with a project, that might’ve just been seen as useful, not hyperactive or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no for sure, because maybe they did and I just didn’t register it, but I don’t have a strong memory of my family making comments about my activity level being unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and restlessness have been present throughout your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.\n\nIt lines up pretty closely with when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That was a big step, and since then it’s felt like my brain just doesn’t shut off the same way. Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and restlessness caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.\n\nAt work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.\n\nAnd with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.\n\nAt home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can really point to. I don’t remember having this kind of focus problem or restlessness before I was 12.\n\nI mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy, but this thing I’m talking about now feels different. It’s been building more in the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I started noticing it as something that was actually affecting me, not something I remember being part of my childhood.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I mean… as certain as I can be, looking back that far. I don’t have some perfect memory of being a kid, so I don’t want to say it like I’ve reviewed every moment of my childhood. But no, I can’t really identify this same kind of thing before age 12.\n\nWhat I’m dealing with now feels tied to the last several months. It’s been building for about seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I really noticed my focus and restlessness becoming something I couldn’t just brush off or explain as normal busyness.\n\nBefore that, I’m sure I had normal kid energy or got distracted sometimes like anyone, but I don’t remember it being this pattern or this level of problem. It started becoming noticeable after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment, and that’s when it went from just normal stress or being busy to something I actually felt was affecting me day to day.\n\nSo, no—I can’t honestly point to anything before age 7. The timeline that makes sense to me is much more recent, around those last seven months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as these focus and restlessness problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t really recall anyone saying that, no. I mean, before I started school is especially hard for me to know, because that’s like… I’d mostly be relying on stories other people told me later, not my own memory.

So I guess the honest answer is I don’t have a specific memory of anyone mentioning that about me from before school age. It’s possible there were comments here and there that I just don’t remember, but nothing clear comes to mind.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… what finally pushed me to come in is I can’t keep my focus the way I need to anymore. I’m an electrician, so a lot of my day is estimates, code forms, keeping track of what materials go with which job, that kind of thing—and lately I’ll sit down to finish one estimate and then a tool noise in the shop, or my phone buzzing, or even just some random new idea I get about reorganizing the workshop will pull me completely off track.\n\nAnd then I’ll realize I’ve mixed up parts of two jobs, or I started a form and wandered off into something else before finishing it. It’s not just “oh, I got distracted once.” It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to mess with my work, and that’s what scared me into making the appointment.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah—restless is probably the right word. I don’t know if it always looks like fidgeting exactly, but I feel like I’m constantly in motion. I’ll go from a job site to doing a bid, then back to the workshop to rewire some test panel I suddenly decided needs fixing, and then I’ll start sketching out some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one.\n\nIt’s like sitting still feels… wrong, almost like I’m wasting time if I’m not doing something useful. Even when I’m supposed to just sit and finish paperwork, my body wants to get up and check tools, move materials around, start another task—something. It’s gotten to where I’m doing a lot, but not necessarily finishing it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or restlessness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can think back and remember being a kid and not loving sitting still in class, but I don’t know if that was “trouble” in the way you mean, or just being a normal kid who wanted to be outside or doing something with his hands.\n\nI don’t have, like, a clear memory of teachers constantly saying, “He can’t focus,” or anything that stands out strongly. School was kind of school—I got through it. I know I was always more interested in practical stuff, building things, taking things apart, that kind of thing, but I don’t know if I’d call that restlessness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or family members ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember them saying much in a clear way. Like, I’m trying to think of the exact kinds of comments my parents made, and nothing really specific comes to mind—nothing like, “You were bouncing off the walls,” or “You never sat still,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nI mean, families say little things, you know? Like maybe I was “always busy” or I liked being outside or messing with stuff, but I don’t know if that was them describing a problem or just normal kid stuff. My dad was pretty practical too, so if I was taking something apart or helping with a project, that might’ve just been seen as useful, not hyperactive or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no for sure, because maybe they did and I just didn’t register it, but I don’t have a strong memory of my family making comments about my activity level being unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and restlessness have been present throughout your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.\n\nIt lines up pretty closely with when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That was a big step, and since then it’s felt like my brain just doesn’t shut off the same way. Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and restlessness caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.\n\nAt work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.\n\nAnd with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.\n\nAt home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can really point to. I don’t remember having this kind of focus problem or restlessness before I was 12.\n\nI mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy, but this thing I’m talking about now feels different. It’s been building more in the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I started noticing it as something that was actually affecting me, not something I remember being part of my childhood.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I mean… as certain as I can be, looking back that far. I don’t have some perfect memory of being a kid, so I don’t want to say it like I’ve reviewed every moment of my childhood. But no, I can’t really identify this same kind of thing before age 12.\n\nWhat I’m dealing with now feels tied to the last several months. It’s been building for about seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I really noticed my focus and restlessness becoming something I couldn’t just brush off or explain as normal busyness.\n\nBefore that, I’m sure I had normal kid energy or got distracted sometimes like anyone, but I don’t remember it being this pattern or this level of problem. The clear change, to me, is much more recent.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain that you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity at any point before the age of 7?\n\nPatient: Before age 7? I really don’t think so. I know I’ve said something similar already, but when I try to look back that far, I just don’t have a memory of being that kind of distracted or restless as a little kid in a way that stood out.\n\nWhat I can say pretty clearly is that this current thing has been building for about seven months. It started becoming noticeable after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment, and that’s when it went from just normal stress or being busy to something I actually felt was affecting me day to day.\n\nSo, no—I can’t honestly point to anything before age 7. The timeline that makes sense to me is much more recent, around those last seven months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as these focus and restlessness problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been stressed, definitely, and the timing kind of overlaps because the focus and restlessness stuff started around a period where there was more pressure in general. But as far as whether it was like a separate depression or anxiety issue… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I don’t want to say yes to something if what I’m describing is just being overwhelmed or under pressure. I’ve noticed the focus and restlessness more clearly than anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus and restlessness issues might be related to feeling overwhelmed or anxious about your work and personal life situation?\n\nPatient: It might be, yeah… I mean, I can see how it could be connected. I just don’t know how to separate it out neatly.\n\nWork and personal stuff have felt like a lot, and when there’s that kind of pressure, I do notice I get more keyed up and it’s harder to settle into one thing. But I’m not sure if that means I’m anxious in the way you’re asking, or if I’m just reacting to having too much on my plate. Like, I don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, it’s definitely anxiety,” because I’m not totally sure that’s what it is.\n\nWhat I do know is that when I feel overwhelmed, the focus issues and restlessness seem harder to ignore. Whether that’s the cause, or it’s just making something else worse, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Are you completely certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity at any point before your 12th birthday?\n\nPatient: Completely certain? I don’t know if I can say completely certain, just because that’s a long time ago and memory gets fuzzy. Before 12 is still pretty far back.\n\nBut I don’t really remember having those kinds of problems then. I wasn’t thinking of myself as a kid who couldn’t sit still or couldn’t pay attention, at least not in any way that stands out to me now. I’m trying to be careful, because maybe there were normal kid moments—getting bored, not wanting to do homework, whatever—but nothing that I can point to and say, “Yes, that was a real pattern before I turned 12.”\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is: I’m not completely certain in the absolute sense, but I don’t have a clear memory of inattention or hyperactivity being an issue before my 12th birthday.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m certain in the way you’re asking, no. I don’t have a strong memory of teachers constantly saying I couldn’t focus, or me feeling like I was always unable to pay attention in a way that stood out. I just don’t have anything specific I can point to from before age 12 that makes me think, yes, that was definitely happening then.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity between the ages of 7 and 12?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know that I can say yes to that. Between 7 and 12, I don’t have a clear memory of having ongoing inattention or hyperactivity symptoms. Like, I’m trying to picture myself at that age—school, being in class, doing homework—and nothing really stands out as a pattern.\n\nI’m sure I had normal kid stuff, like getting bored or not wanting to sit through something, but I don’t remember it being something people were worried about or something that caused a lot of problems. I don’t remember thinking, “I can’t focus,” or being told all the time that I was too restless.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not that I can clearly remember. I wouldn’t want to swear there was absolutely nothing, because I was a kid and it’s hard to know, but I don’t have anything specific from ages 7 to 12 that I’d point to and say, yeah, that was definitely happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you recall your parents or anyone else mentioning that you had trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were really young, like before you started school?\n\nPatient: I don’t really recall anyone saying that, no. I’ll go from a job site to doing a bid, then back to the workshop to rewire some test panel I suddenly decided needs fixing, and then I’ll start sketching out some home-automation idea before I’ve even finished the last one.\n\nIt’s like sitting still feels… wrong, almost like I’m wasting time if I’m not doing something useful. Even when I’m supposed to just sit and finish paperwork, my body wants to get up and check tools, move materials around, start another task—something. It’s gotten to where I’m doing a lot, but not necessarily finishing it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble with focus or restlessness when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I can think back and remember being a kid and not loving sitting still in class, but I don’t know if that was “trouble” in the way you mean, or just being a normal kid who wanted to be outside or doing something with his hands.\n\nI don’t have, like, a clear memory of teachers constantly saying, “He can’t focus,” or anything that stands out strongly. School was kind of school—I got through it. I know I was always more interested in practical stuff, building things, taking things apart, that kind of thing, but I don’t know if I’d call that restlessness exactly.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. It’s not something I’ve really thought about before, and nothing obvious is jumping out at me from childhood.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or family members ever comment on your behavior or activity level when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t remember them saying much in a clear way. Like, I’m trying to think of the exact kinds of comments my parents made, and nothing really specific comes to mind—nothing like, “You were bouncing off the walls,” or “You never sat still,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nI mean, families say little things, you know? Like maybe I was “always busy” or I liked being outside or messing with stuff, but I don’t know if that was them describing a problem or just normal kid stuff. My dad was pretty practical too, so if I was taking something apart or helping with a project, that might’ve just been seen as useful, not hyperactive or anything.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say no for sure, because maybe they did and I just didn’t register it, but I don’t have a strong memory of my family making comments about my activity level being unusual.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with focus and restlessness have been present throughout your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more recent. Not like it started yesterday or anything, but when I look back, the last… maybe seven months or so is when it really started building.\n\nIt lines up pretty closely with when we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That was a big step, and since then it’s felt like my brain just doesn’t shut off the same way. Before that I could be busy, sure, and I’ve always liked having projects going, but this level of being pulled from one thing to the next and feeling like I can’t settle—this feels like it’s been ramping up since then.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s been exactly the same my whole life. I don’t have that clear childhood-to-now pattern. It feels more like something that’s grown over the past several months, especially with the pressure of the workshop and equipment hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties with focus and restlessness caused you significant distress or problems in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I wish I could say it’s just work stress and it stays at the shop, but it doesn’t. It’s starting to spill into everything.\n\nAt work, clients have been catching things I should’ve caught—missed details, wrong notes, scheduling stuff getting mixed up. And that’s embarrassing, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be solid. People are trusting you to know what’s happening and keep things straight. I’ve had a couple clients complain, not just casually mention it, but actually be frustrated with me. That gets in my head.\n\nAnd with my apprentices… I can feel it there too. They used to just kind of follow my lead, and now I can tell they’re second-guessing me. Like if I tell them where we’re supposed to be or what needs to happen next, there’s this hesitation, like they’re wondering if I’ve got it right. That bothers me more than I expected, honestly, because I don’t want to be the guy people have to work around.\n\nAt home it’s been rough too. The mortgage, the business, the equipment—all of that has turned into this constant argument with my wife. She feels like I’m putting everything we built at risk, and I get why she says that, even if I don’t like hearing it. It’s like we can’t talk about normal things without it circling back to money or the shop or whether I’m handling things the right way.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing problems. It’s not just me feeling distracted. It’s affecting people’s trust in me, and it’s affecting my marriage.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any of these focus or restlessness issues before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: No, not that I can really point to. I don’t remember having this kind of focus problem or restlessness before I was 12.\n\nI mean, I was a kid, so I’m sure I had normal kid energy, but this thing I’m talking about now feels different. It’s been building more in the last seven months or so, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. That’s when I started noticing it as something that was actually affecting me, not something I remember being part of my childhood.\n\nDoctor: Are you certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: I mean… as certain as I can be, looking back that far. I don’t have some perfect memory of being a kid, so I don’t want to say it like I’ve reviewed every moment of my childhood. But no, I can’t really identify this same kind of thing before age 12.\n\nWhat I’m dealing with now feels tied to the last several months. It’s been building for about seven months, since we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment. It started becoming noticeable after we took on the mortgage for the workshop and the equipment, and that’s when it went from just normal stress or being busy to something I actually felt was affecting me day to day.\n\nSo, no—I can’t honestly point to anything before age 7. The timeline that makes sense to me is much more recent, around those last seven months.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other mental health issues, such as depression or anxiety, around the same time as these focus and restlessness problems started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I’ve been stressed, definitely, and the timing kind of overlaps because the focus and restlessness stuff started around a period where there was more pressure in general. But as far as whether it was like a separate depression or anxiety issue… I don’t know.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it that way. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I don’t want to say yes to something if what I’m describing is just being overwhelmed or under pressure. I’ve noticed the focus and restlessness more clearly than anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your focus and restlessness issues might be related to feeling overwhelmed or anxious about your work and personal life situation?\n\nPatient: It might be, yeah… I mean, I can see how it could be connected. I just don’t know how to separate it out neatly.\n\nWork and personal stuff have felt like a lot, and when there’s that kind of pressure, I do notice I get more keyed up and it’s harder to settle into one thing. Like, I don’t want to overstate it and say, “Yes, it’s definitely anxiety,” because I’m not totally sure that’s what it is.\n\nWhat I do know is that when I feel overwhelmed, the focus issues and restlessness seem harder to ignore. Whether that’s the cause, or it’s just making something else worse, I honestly don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Are you completely certain you didn't experience any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity at any point before your 12th birthday?\n\nPatient: Completely certain? I don’t know if I can say completely certain, just because that’s a long time ago and memory gets fuzzy. Before the last seven months? I’m not really sure I’d say I was experiencing that, at least not in a way that I noticed as a clear thing.
